115年 10月賦稅收入初步統計 
一、整體賦稅收入 
（一）實徵淨額 
10 月實徵淨額 5,669 億元，較上年同月增加 1,495 億元；累計 1 至
10 月實徵 4 兆 1,383 億元，較上年同期增加 8,499 億元(+25.8%)，
主要由證交稅、營所稅及營業稅所貢獻。隨新版財政收支劃分法實
施，營業稅劃歸中央政府比率由 61.2%降為 4.5%(獲配稅額隨之減
少 2,662 億元)，致中央政府稅收增加數 3,906 億元(+15.8%)，不及
地方政府增加數 4,671 億元(+68.5%)。 
（二）預算達成率 
累 計1 至 10 月 實 徵 淨 額 占 全 年 預 算 數 91.4% ， 其 中 中 央 政 府 為
92.8%，地方政府為87.7%；與全年預算數差距分別為3,913億元、
2,227億元、1,614億元。 
圖 1 賦稅實徵淨額及年增率

說明：115 年 5-7 月年增率跳動較大，係因 114 年所得稅結算申報及繳納期限延長至 6 月底所致。

二、主要稅目別變動
（一）10 月實徵淨額與上年同月比較，以營利事業所得稅增加 1,133 億元、
證券交易稅增加 220 億元及綜合所得稅增加 120 億元較多。
（二）累計 1 至 10 月實徵淨額與上年同期比較，以證券交易稅增加 3,297
億元、營利事業所得稅增加 2,986 億元及營業稅增加 1,272 億元較多。
圖 2 主要稅目別變化
(115 年 1-10 月)
1.營利事業所得稅
(1)10 月實徵淨額 4,144 億元，較上年同月增加 1,133 億元(+37.6%)，
主因暫繳稅款成長。
(2)累計 1 至 10 月實徵淨額 1 兆 4,172 億元，較上年同期增加 2,986
億元(+26.7%)，主因結算申報自繳、暫繳稅款俱增及延分期稅款
陸續入帳。
2.綜合所得稅
10 月實徵淨額 614 億元，較上年同月增加 120 億元(+24.3%)，主因
扣繳稅款增加所致；累計 1 至 10 月實徵淨額 8,894 億元，較上年同
期增加 910 億元(+11.4%)，主因結算申報自繳稅額增加，以及盈餘分

配、薪資所得等扣繳稅款均呈成長。
3.營業稅
(1)10月受進口代徵稅額增加及適用外銷零稅率營業人申請退稅增加
之交互影響，營業稅實徵淨額為負77億元，較上年同月(負146億元)
增加69億元。
(2)累 計1 至10 月 實 徵 淨額6,153 億 元 ， 較 上年 同 期 增 加1,272 億元
(+26.1%)，主因內需穩定，資通與視聽產品、電子零組件等進口
代徵稅額增加，惟外銷營業人申請退稅亦增，抵銷部分增幅。

4.貨物稅
10月實徵淨額73億元，較上年同月減少41億元(-35.6%)，累計1至10
月實徵淨額948億元，較上年同期減少248億元(-20.7%)，主因新購汽
機車及電動車之減免徵退稅增加，加以汽柴油減徵幅度擴大所致。
5.證券交易稅
(1)10 月及累計 1 至 10 月上市上櫃股票平均每日成交值為 1 兆 1,210
億元、1 兆 1,437 億元，分別較上年同期(6,267 億元、4,632 億元)
增 78.9%、1.5 倍。
(2)10 月證交稅實徵淨額 523 億元， 較上年同月增加 220 億元
(+72.6%)；累計 1 至 10 月實徵淨額 5,583 億元，較上年同期增加
3,297 億元(+1.4 倍)。
6.關稅
10 月實徵淨額 132 億元，較上年同月增加 7 億元(+6.0%)，累計 1 至
10 月實徵淨額 1,356 億元，較上年同期增加 88 億元(+6.9%)，均因機
械及電機設備、基本金屬、塑橡膠及其製品等進口稅額增加。
7.贈與稅
10 月實徵淨額 23 億元，較上年同月減少 11 億元(-31.9%)，主因大額

稅款案件減少；累計 1 至 10 月實徵淨額 234 億元，較上年同期增加
32 億元(+15.9%)，主因大額稅款案件增加。
8.土地增值稅
10 月實徵淨額 56 億元，較上年同月增加 3 億元(+5.9%)，主因大額
稅款案件增加；累計 1 至 10 月實徵淨額 560 億元，較上年同期減少
11 億元(-2.0%)，主因房地交易量及大額稅款案件減少。
9.使用牌照稅
10 月實徵淨額 6 億元，較上年同月減少 1 億元(-10.2%)，累計 1 至
10 月實徵淨額 676 億元，較上年同期減少 1 億元(-0.1%)，主因開徵
輛數減少，以及稅款入帳時間差異所致。
（三）前 10月主要稅目實徵淨額占全年預算數之比率：
1.大於 100%(已提前達成全年預算目標)者有遺產稅(123%)、贈與稅
(107%)、印花稅(106%)、房屋稅(105%)及營所稅(102%)。
2.低於 100%者有牌照稅(99%)、營業稅(94%)、綜所稅(93%)、契稅
(90%)、關稅(88%)、菸酒稅(81%)、證交稅(80%)、貨物稅(79%)、土

增稅(71%)及特銷稅(47%)等。

三、政府別賦稅收入概況
(一)中央政府
累計 1 至 10 月中央政府賦稅收入(不含中央統籌分配稅款)實徵淨
額 2 兆 8,579 億元(占全年預算數 92.8%)，較上年同期增加 3,906 億元
(+15.8%)，主因新版財政收支劃分法施行，營業稅劃歸中央政府比率
大幅調降，獲配稅額隨之減少 2,662 億元，惟證交稅、所得稅分別增加
3,297 億元、3,306 億元，互抵後呈現上升。
(二)地方政府
累計 1 至 10 月地方政府賦稅
收入(含中央統籌分配稅款)實
徵淨額 1 兆 1,487 億元(占全年
預算數 87.7%)，較上年同期增
加 4,671 億元(+68.5%)，主因
納入中央統籌分配之營業稅
增加 4,013 億元所致。
(三)中央特種基金
累計 1 至 10 月撥入中央特種基金之健康福利捐實徵淨額 220 億元，較
上年同期增加 4 億元(+1.7%)；房地合一課徵所得稅撥入住宅基金 144
億元，較上年同期減少 6 億元(-4.0%)；撥入長照基金 953 億元(其中 575

億元來自房地合一課徵所得稅、220 億元來自菸稅，158 億元來自遺贈
稅)，較上年同期增加 2 億元(+0.2%)。

Document 062: 2026年10月经济数据点评：新兴产业与外需支撑景气，内需修复持续承压

Source PDF: all report pdf/2026年10月经济数据点评：新兴产业与外需支撑景气，内需修复持续承压.pdf
Report source: 西南证券
Date: 2026-11-18
Pages: 7
Status: ok

### Page 1

请务必阅读正文后的重要声明部分  
1 
[Table_ReportInfo] 2026 年 11 月 16 日      
证 券研究报告•宏观简评报告     
数据点评 
新兴产业与外需支撑景气，内需修复持续承压 
——2026 年 10 月经济数据点评

点评

西 南证券研究院 
[Table_Summary]  摘要：工业生产方面，10 月规模以上工业增加值同比增长 4.8%，增速较上月
回落 0.7 个百分点，低于市场预期。高技术制造业和装备制造业增加值分别增
长 17.6%、10.6%，其中高技术制造业创近五年来单月增速新高，AI相关电子
硬件、工业机器人、3D 打印设备等产品产量保持较快增长。同时，10 月规上
工业出口交货值增长 18.6%，外需仍是工业生产的重要支撑。整体工业生产呈
现总量放缓、结构分化的特征，高技术制造和出口链条景气度较高，传统周期
性行业修复相对滞后。固定资产投资方面，1-10 月全国固定资产投资同比下降
8.1%，降幅较 1-9 月扩大 0.9 个百分点，投资需求仍处于深度收缩区间。基建
投资方面，1-10 月广义基建投资下降 5.1%，专项债发行节奏放缓、资金向实
物工作量转化存在时滞，整体托底效应尚未充分显现，但互联网和相关服务业
投资，以及交通运输、电力供应等重点领域投资仍保持正增长。随着专项债剩
余额度加快使用及“两重”项目建设推进，重点基建领域投资有望获得一定支
撑，但扭转整体投资下行趋势仍面临较大难度。房地产投资方面，1-10 月房地
产开发投资同比下降 21.6%，降幅较 1-9 月扩大 1.0 个百分点，地产仍是整体
投资的重要拖累。供给端施工、竣工面积单月同比分别下降 32.5%、39.1%，
降幅均有所扩大，新开工降幅有所收窄但仍处负增长区间；需求端商品房销售
面积、销售额分别下降 20.7%、20.5%，降幅小幅收窄，但销售修复尚未有效
传导至房企投资和施工活动。房企资金来源继续收缩，行业投资意愿和项目建
设规模仍然偏弱，预计年内地产投资仍处深度调整阶段，降幅收窄时点或推迟
至 2027 年。制造业投资方面，1-10 月制造业投资同比下降 2.9%，降幅较 1-9
月扩大 0.8 个百分点，企业资本开支意愿仍然偏弱。高技术制造业投资增长
3.0%，电子电路、锂离子电池和集成电路制造业投资增速较快，科技产业投资
仍具韧性。“两新”政策继续支撑设备更新，设备工器具购置投资增长 8.9%，
占全部投资比重提升至 20.3%。但汽车、通用设备等装备制造行业投资明显走
弱，反映生产端韧性尚未转化为新增资本开支。消费方面，10 月社会消费品
零售总额同比增长 0.4%，增速较上月回落 0.7 个百分点，消费需求整体偏弱。
汽车类零售同比下降 21.4%，仍是社零增长的主要拖累；通讯器材、金银珠宝
等品类受前期高基数及金价波动影响，零售增速明显回落，家电、家具消费则
受低基数影响边际改善。服务消费相对更具韧性，1-10 月服务零售额增长
4.9%，高于商品零售额 4.0 个百分点。往后看，汽车及地产相关消费能否改善、
以旧换新政策效应能否延续，仍是影响商品消费修复的重要因素，服务消费则
有望继续成为居民消费的重要支撑。 
 工业生产总量边际降温，科技制造与出口链条维持高景气。2026 年 10 月，全
国规模以上工业增加值同比增长 4.8%，增速较上月收窄 0.7 个百分点，低于
wind 一致预期的 5.32%。分行业看，采矿业、制造业、电力、热力、燃气及
水生产和供应业的工业增加值同比较上月均边际走弱，增速分别回落至 0.7%、
5.4%、4.4%。但制造业工业增加值同比相比近十年季节性水平略高，表明生
产端并未出现明显失速。结构上，10 月高技术产业、装备制造业工业增加值
同比分别增长 17.6%、10.6%。高技术产业增速创下近 5 年来单月最高增速，
其中电子工业专用设备制造、计算机外围设备制造、电子器件制造等行业增加
值分别增长 46.8%、44.7%、34.8%，显示电子信息产业链仍处于较高景气区
间。装备制造业 1-10 月累计增加值占全部规模以上工业比重为 37.9%，同比
提高 1.8 个百分点。细分行业中，10 月单月，计算机、通信和其他电子设备制

2
造业、专用设备制造业、石油和天然气开采业、铁路、船舶、航空航天和其他
运输设备制造业增加值同比分别增长 19.4%、11.5%、9.4%、9.2%，在主要
行业中增幅居前。金属制品业（8.6%）、食品制造业（8.6%）、医药制造业（5.9%）
增速均边际回升。非金属矿物制品业（-2.3%）、煤炭开采和洗选业（-4.4%）
同比降幅均小幅收窄。这反映出工业生产的改善并非全面扩散，传统周期性行
业仍受到需求不足、价格偏弱等因素制约，产业间景气分化较为明显。从产品
看，“人工智能+”带动产业链上下游产量较快增长，既体现在集成电路
（+21.9%）、电子元件（+15.2%）等核心硬件持续扩张，也体现在工业机器人
（+26.0%）、 3D 打印设备（+85.9%）等高端制造设备加快放量，以及地面通
信导航定向设备（+61.5%）等智能终端产品快速增长，反映出 AI产业发展正
由底层硬件向智能制造和多元化应用场景延伸。出口亦对工业生产形成正向带
动。10 月规模以上工业企业出口交货值同比增长 18.6%，增速较上月加快 1.6

个百分点。装备制造业出口交货值增长 20.6%，增速较上月加快 3.4 个百分点，
其中，汽车、电气机械、电子行业出口交货值分别增长 52.2%、23.7%、15.3%，
对全部规上工业出口交货值增长的贡献率居各大类行业前三位，合计达
66.7%，说明出口对工业生产的拉动并未明显减弱，且支撑力量主要集中在装
备制造和电子产业链。综合来看，年内工业增加值增速或温和放缓，结构升级
趋势明确，高技术制造业和装备制造业是核心支撑。在强出口和稳投资政策发
力下，单月增速有望小幅回升，但难以扭转全年较前三季度小幅放缓的格局。
 整体投资降幅进一步扩大，重点基建领域维持较高景气。2026 年 1-10 月，全
国固定资产投资（不含农户）同比下降 8.1%，降幅较 1-9 月扩大 0.9 个百分
点。根据历史数据倒推测算，10 月单月投资同比下降 17.84%，降幅较上月扩
大 8.16 个百分点。累计降幅扩大相对温和，但单月降幅明显加深，反映近期
投资下行压力仍在加大。基建投资方面，1-10 月广义基建投资同比下降 5.1%，

降幅较 1-9 月扩大 0.9 个百分点；根据历史数据倒推测算，10 月单月广义基建
投资同比下降 17.63%，降幅较上月扩大 7.15 个百分点。资金端来看，10 月
地方政府新增专项债发行 3280 亿元，同比涨幅收窄至 14.13%，发行进度
88.07%，慢于去年同期 2.03 个百分点。专项债发行节奏放缓，叠加前期资金
向项目建设转化存在时滞，基建投资尚未形成回升趋势。尽管广义基建投资整
体负增长，但重点领域投资仍保持较快增长。1-10 月互联网和相关服务业投资
同比增长 48.4%，增速较 1-9 月份加快 1.7 个百分点，反映数字基础设施建设
仍有较强投资需求；航空运输业、水上运输业投资均增长 12.5%，电力供应业
投资增长 9.9%，说明“两重”项目建设及“六张网”相关领域投资仍在推进。
往后看，在四季度财政资金集中拨付、重大项目加速形成实物工作量的支撑下，
11、12 月单月投资增速环比有望小幅改善，带动全年降幅较 1-10 月略有收窄，
但全年转正的概率较低。基建投资中部分结构性高增领域可能受低基数、项目

集中开工影响，持续性需观察。同时，专项债剩余额度在年内可能对基建形成
一定支撑，但不宜高估其扭转深度负增的能力。
 地产供需两端低位运行，投资意愿与在建规模仍偏弱。2026 年 1-10 月，全国
房地产开发投资同比下降 21.6%，降幅较 1-9 月扩大 1.0 个百分点；据历史数
据倒推测算，10月单月房地产开发投资同比下降 34.24%，降幅较上月扩大 7.04
个百分点，地产对整体投资的拖累有所加深，是投资端最大拖累项之一。供给
端，根据历史数据倒推测算，10 月房屋施工面积同比下降 32.5%，降幅较上
月扩大 13.9 个百分点，反映在建规模明显收缩；新开工面积同比下降 18.0%，
降幅收窄 8.1 个百分点，是供给端唯一边际改善项。新开工绝对增速仍深度负
增，且可能受低基数、项目集中开工影响，房企投资意愿仍未实质性修复；竣
工面积同比下降 39.1%，降幅扩大 2.4 个百分点，反映项目交付端压力或边际
上升。需求端，根据历史数据倒推测算，10 月商品房销售面积同比下降 20.7%，

降幅较上月收窄 1.9 个百分点；销售额同比下降 20.5%，降幅收窄 0.6 个百分
点。销售额降幅收窄幅度小于销售面积，意味着销售均价同比表现相对偏弱，
销售端的量价修复仍不充分。同时，销售端边际改善与投资、施工、竣工端恶
化形成背离。说明销售回款尚未有效转化为房企拿地、开工和施工强度，房企
流动性和投资意愿仍是核心约束。资金端，据历史数据倒推测算，10 月单月
房地产开发资金来源同比下降 22.0%，降幅较上月进一步扩大。其中国内贷款、
自筹资金、定金及预收款分别下降 33.0%、22.0%、15.6%，资金链仍在全面

### Page 3

请务必阅读正文后的重要声明部分
3
收缩。往后看，年内稳地产政策以公积金改革、核心城市限购优化、购房贴息
等需求端工具为主，供给端主要依赖收储去库存，年底地产投资降幅仍在寻顶，
降幅收窄时点或推迟到 2027 年。
 整体企业资本开支意愿偏弱，高技术制造投资稳健增长。2026 年 1-10 月，制
造业投资同比下降 2.9%，降幅较 1-9 月扩大 0.8 个百分点；据历史数据倒推
测算，10 月单月制造业投资同比下降 9.32%，降幅较上月扩大 8.38 个百分点。
结合本月工业生产数据看，当前生产端的增长尚未充分转化为企业新增资本开
支，制造业投资意愿相对谨慎。分行业看，1-10 月高技术制造业投资同比增长
3.0%，其中电子电路制造业投资增长 59.6%，锂离子电池、集成电路制造业
投资分别增长 18.0%、13.8%；高技术服务业投资增长 5.9%，其中与人工智
能等新兴产业密切相关的信息服务业投资增长 20.5%，显示科技产业投资持续
向电子信息制造和人工智能相关服务领域集中。“两新”政策持续落地显效，

设备购置投资规模不断扩大。1-10 月，设备工器具购置投资同比增长 8.9%；
占全部投资的比重为 20.3%，比上年同期提高 3.2 个百分点。10 月单月，金属
制品业、有色金属冶炼及压延加工业投资增速同比分别下降 8.5%和 2.5%，降
幅较上月分别收窄 8.2 和 6.8 个百分点。铁路、船舶、航空航天和其他运输设
备制造业、汽车制造业、通用设备制造业当月投资同比分别下降 0.2%、21.8%、
21.2%，降幅分别扩大 12.7、13.6、23.5 个百分点。装备制造业内部投资分化
加剧，终端需求偏弱、价格竞争加剧、产能利用率不足，以及前期抢出口和“以
旧换新”带来的需求前置，可能共同影响企业扩产意愿，促使企业资本开支趋
于谨慎。对比来看，10 月装备制造业工业增加值仍增长 10.6%，反映出当前
装备制造业呈现生产保持较强韧性、投资扩张意愿不足的特征，生产端与投资
端的背离进一步加深。往后看，高技术制造业投资动能有望延续，四季度制造
业投资单月同比有望转正，但全年增速明显转正难度较大；设备购置、电子电

路、锂电、集成电路和 AI 相关信息服务是主要支撑，汽车、通用设备和传统
制造或成为主要拖累。
 消费总量增长继续放缓，部分品类受到价格和低基数扰动。10 月单月，社会
消费品零售总额同比增长 0.4%，增速较上月收窄 0.7 个百分点；扣除汽车后
的消费品零售额同比增长 2.5%，增速较上月收窄 1.4 个百分点。分项看，商
品零售同比增长 0.3%，增速较上月收窄 0.7 个百分点；餐饮收入同比增长
0.7%，增速收窄 1.4 个百分点，整体消费需求仍偏弱。分品类看，汽车类零售
额同比下降 21.4%，降幅较上月扩大 0.2 个百分点，成为社零增长的主要拖累，
反映居民大额耐用品消费意愿不足。通讯器材类零售同比增长 6.5%，增速较
上月大幅回落 24.6 个百分点，说明前期高增长后基数效应明显，补贴效应和
换机需求均有所减弱。金银珠宝类零售同比下降 28.7%，降幅较上月扩大 19.1
个百分点，主要因近期金价回调压低零售额读数，价格因素大于需求因素，也
反映出金银珠宝消费对金价波动高度敏感。石油及制品类零售同比由下降转为

上升 4.4%，主要受近期油价上涨叠加基数因素影响。家用电器和音像器材类
零售同比上升 2.5%，家具类零售同比下降 2.6%，增速均边际回升，或受去年
“以旧换新”退潮带来的低基数影响。尽管消费总量增速有所回落，消费结构
升级和服务消费韧性仍在延续。1-10 月，社会消费品和服务零售总额同比增长
2.5%，其中服务零售额增长 4.9%，高于商品零售额 4.0 个百分点。往后看，
以旧换新政策边际效应减弱、居民消费意愿回升偏慢等因素或持续拖累社零整
体增速。年内消费韧性更多取决于服务消费能否延续较快增速，以及即时零售、
银发经济等新增长点能否对冲传统商品消费偏弱的拖累，托举社零中枢维持在
温和区间。
 风险提示：政策落地节奏不及预期风险，海外经济超预期波动风险。已公布的
宏观或产业政策在实际执行过程中，可能因配套措施不完善、部门协调不畅、
地方执行力度差异或市场环境变化等因素，导致其推进速度、覆盖范围或最终
效果慢于或弱于市场预期，从而影响政策目标的实现与经济修复的进程；主要
经济体的政治局势、经济数据或宏观政策出现意外变化，其幅度或频率超出市

场普遍预测。这种超预期波动可能引发全球金融市场动荡、贸易条件恶化或外
需急剧收缩，并通过金融、贸易及预期渠道向国内传导，增加我国外部环境的
复杂性与不稳定性。

分析师承诺 
本报告署名分析师具有中国证券业协会授予的证券投资咨询执业资格并注册为证券分析师，报告所采用的数据均
来自合法合规渠道，分析逻辑基于分析师的职业理解，通过合理判断得出结论，独立、客观地出具本报告。分析师承
诺不曾因，不因，也将不会因本报告中的具体推荐意见或观点而直接或间接获取任何形式的补偿。

投资评级说明 
报告中投资建议所涉及的评级分为公司评级和行业评级（另有说明的除外）。评级标准为报告发布日后 6 个月内
的相对市场表现，即：以报告发布日后 6 个月内公司股价（或行业指数）相对同期相关证券市场代表性指数的涨跌幅
作为基准。其中：A 股市场以沪深 300 指数为基准，新三板市场以三板成指（针对协议转让标的）或三板做市指数（针
对做市转让标的）为基准；香港市场以恒生指数为基准；美国市场以纳斯达克综合指数或标普 500 指数为基准。 
公司评级 
买入：未来 6 个月内，个股相对同期相关证券市场代表性指数涨幅在 20%以上 
持有：未来 6 个月内，个股相对同期相关证券市场代表性指数涨幅介于 10%与 20%之间 
中性：未来 6 个月内，个股相对同期相关证券市场代表性指数涨幅介于-10%与 10%之间 
回避：未来 6 个月内，个股相对同期相关证券市场代表性指数涨幅介于-20%与-10%之间 
卖出：未来 6 个月内，个股相对同期相关证券市场代表性指数涨幅在-20%以下 
行业评级

Greater China Technology Hardware
Asia Pacific
Industry View In-Line
In this report, we focus on preliminary JMTBA orders, which we
believe could be a catalyst for AirTAC's and Hiwin's share prices.
According to the Japan Machine Tool Builders' Association (JMTBA), headline

orders dropped 8% MoM but grew 43% YoY in October  2026 (vs. +13% MoM/
+60% YoY in September 2026), to ¥204bn.
• Domestic orders were -18% MoM/+47% YoY (vs. +24% MoM/+46% YoY in
September), at ¥52bn.
• Overseas orders were -5% MoM/+41% YoY (vs. +9% MoM/+67% YoY  in
September), at ¥152bn.
Our view:
• For overseas orders, the growth remains solid on a YoY basis. We think the
sequential decline is likely from China as its manufacturing PMI edged down

to 49.3 in Oct (vs. 50.1 in Sep). Meanwhile, North America and Europe could
prove more resilient, with manufacturing PMIs staying relatively firm at
55.5/52.7 in Oct (vs. 54.5/52.9 in Sep)
• We remain OW on both Airtac (1590.TW) and Hiwin (2049.TW), as we see
company-specific drivers on top of the industry recovery trend:
° Airtac should continue to benefit from its own market share gains.
Valuation also appears undemanding at 15x 2027e P/E (vs. 25x average

since 2020).
° Hiwin should  see margin expansion in the coming quarters, driven by
higher utilization and price hikes. Current valuation of 27x 2027 P/E
implies upside vs. peak cycle valuation at 35-40x P/E.
Morgan Stanley does and seeks to do business with
companies covered in Morgan Stanley Research. As a result,
investors should be aware that the firm may have a conflict of
interest that could affect the objectivity of Morgan Stanley
Research. Investors should consider Morgan Stanley

communications with a subject company, public appearances
and trading securities held by a research analyst account.

Morgan Stanley Research 3
Valuation Methodology and Risks
AirTAC International (1590.TW)
Base case, 25x 2027e P/E. We believe this methodology better captures earnings growth
momentum for industrial automation component plays (market sentiment moves along
with business cycles). We think our valuation of 25x P/E is reasonable (vs. average of 25x since
2020).
Risks to Upside
n Stronger macroeconomic climate in China
n Stronger-than-expected demand from industrial automation

Risks to Downside
n Sharper economic downturn in China
n Miniature linear guideway business development taking more time
n Larger-than-expected investment in new products but weaker-than-expected demand
Hiwin Technologies Corp. (2049.TW)
Base case, P/E. We think this valuation methodology better captures Hiwin's earnings growth
momentum, as its valuation tends to move with the business cycle. We apply a target multiple
of 34x to our 2027 EPS estimate. We view this multiple as justified by the 50% operating profit

CAGR we project for 2025-28.
Risks to Upside
n Stronger industrial automation demand pickup
n Market share gains
n Earlier-than-expected revenue contribution from humanoids
Risks to Downside
n Weaker industrial automation demand
n More pricing pressure
n Market share loss
n More broad-based impact on the global economy from geopolitical risks

Analyst Stock Ratings
Overweight (O). The stock's total return is expected to exceed the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next
12-18 months.
Equal-weight (E). The stock's total return is expected to be in line with the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over

the next 12-18 months.
Not-Rated (NR). Currently the analyst does not have adequate conviction about the stock's total return relative to the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage
universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months.
Underweight (U). The stock's total return is expected to be below the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next

12-18 months.
Unless otherwise specified, the time frame for price targets included in Morgan Stanley Research is 12 to 18 months.
Analyst Industry Views
Attractive (A): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be attractive vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated
below.
In-Line (I): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be in line with the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.

Cautious (C): The analyst views the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months with caution vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.
Benchmarks for each region are as follows: North America - S&P 500; Latin America - relevant MSCI country index or MSCI Latin America Index; Europe - MSCI Europe; Japan - TOPIX; Asia -
relevant MSCI country index or MSCI sub-regional index or MSCI AC Asia Pacific ex Japan Index.

### Page 1

Technology - Asia-Pacific (H)

ABF substrate & CCL: making the case for 
limited downside after valuation reset

Price Objective Change

Valuation implies sizable 2028 EPS downside   
Broadly speaking, share prices of the ABF substrate and CCL stocks under our Greater 
China coverage fell by 40-50% from their October peaks to their November lows. Based 
on the two scenarios presented in this report, we believe current valuations may imply 
the market’s over-concerns about the inflection of AI server cycle or a slowdown in 
capex growth. Using closing price as of Nov. 16th, the stocks traded at 7-13x 2028E P/E 
under scenario 1, in which we assume a 15% shortfall in AI-related revenue vs. our 
estimates (Exhibit 3). These multiples are below the midpoint of their valuation range 
before the rise of AI server demand from mid-2023. Under scenario 2, we divide the 
current share price by our long-term anchored P/E multiple to calculate the 2028E EPS 
imlied by the share price. Based on our analysis, current valuations imply a 40-90% 
shortfall (or discount) in AI-related revenue versus our estimates (Exhibit 4). 
Raise estimates but lower P/E multiples  
For 2027-28E OP, we raise our estimates by 4-9%/19-31%/3-6%/19-24%/22-23%/32-
33% for Unimicron/Kinsus/NYPCB/EMC/TUC/Shengyi, reflecting a general beat in 3Q26 
GM and ongoing price hike outlook across ABF/BT substrate and CCL. However, we 
lower our POs on these names (Exhibit 1) as we lower P/E multiples by ~30% to reflect 
sector-wide de-rating in AI server supply chain, while the valuation period is unchanged. 
See Exhibit 10 for details of the changes in valuation multiples.  
Stay largely positive on ABF substrate and CCL sectors 
Despite lowered POs and multiples, our positive stance on the ABF substrate sector is 
unchanged, reflecting our expectation of a more sustained supply deficit through 2028 
as well as the T-glass price hike by Nittobo (see report) and Resonac, which should set 
the stage for substrate makers to lift ASP further. We thus reiterate Buy on Unimicron, 
Kinsus and NYPCB. As for CCL, we are also generally positive on the S/D outlook given 
the lead time of certain equipment is still at least 1.5-2 years, yet at single stock level 
we are more constructive on Buy-rated names including 1) EMC with at least 70-80% 
revenue mix from M7-grade and above in 2027-28 and its estimated 50-60% share of 
the AI-related high-end CCL market, along with potential exposure to M10 adoption in 
SpaceX’s AI server rack based on our channel checks; and 2) TUC being one of the few 
high-end CCL suppliers with more aggressive capacity expansion during 2026-30 (~3x 
over the period). We also see potential for additional ~2mn sheet/month capacity 
addition in 2030 (in Thailand).   For discussions on ABF substrate S/D model update and 
our thoughts on CPO impact, see page 2.

### Page 2

ABF substrate – wider undersupply expected vs previously  
We update ABF substrate industry S/D model with this report, and we now expect 
7%/18%/20% under-supply (was 7%/14%/19%) in 2026/27/28 thanks to the raised TAM 
(see report, Core Wars: raising CPU TAM to $240bn, still early stages of agents) for AI 
accelerators and CPUs (central processing units), which leads us to estimate 36%/24% 
(was 32%/26%) demand growth by the industry in 2027/28. After reflecting the demand 
updates, AI accelerators are expected to account for 41%/52%/55% (was 
40%/46%/51%) of demand in 2026/27/28. Overall, our industry S/D model also 
considers the 2.5x SAP (semi additive process) demand growth across 2026-30 shared 
by Ibiden, which incorporates 1.5x and 4.4x demand growth by server CPU and AI 
accelerators respectively over the same period.  
Thoughts on CPO impact for CCL/PCB 
Our checks with supply chain suggest that many of the CCL materials are upgraded to 
M9-grade (based on low Dk2 cloth and hyper very low profile 4 copper foil), when 
pluggable optical modules are upgraded to OBO (on-board optics) and/or NPO (near 
package optics). In terms of CCL-grade vs PCB layer count, there is a general 
categorization in AI server and adjacent applications shown in Exhibit 12, and it indicates 
that M8-grade or above CCL will be required when the board layer count moves up to 30 
or higher. Further, given that CPO gen-2 with OOI (optics on interposer) is unlikely to 
ramp before 2028, alongside the upgrade in transmission to 3.2T, CCL material used in 
mainboard may have already been upgraded to M10-grade by then under a non- 
CPO/OOI architecture. This means CPO/OOI adoption may only result in a material 
downgrade to M8/M9-grade.

Price objective basis & risk 
Elite Material (ETMCF) 
Our PO of NT$8,200 is based on 30x 2H27-1H28E P/E, at the mid-to-high-end of its 
historical range of 6-34x since 2016. In our view, the multiple is appropriate, given the 
market-share expansion in server/networking, where we see structural growth in 
addressable market.

Upside risks are (1) share gain at substrate CCL, (2) stronger 5G smartphone shipment, 
(3) greater 5G smartphone spec upgrades, and (4) favorable FX and raw material cost 
change.

### Page 13

Downside risks are (1) share loss at iPhone, (2) keener competition in high-end CCL, and 
(3) unfavorable FX and raw material cost change. 
Kinsus (KNSUF) 
We set our PO at NT$1,450 based on 35x 2H27-1H28E P/E, which is at mid-to-high-end 
level in its historical trading range (8-40x) since 2013. In our view, the multiple is backed 
by ROE and operating margin recovery to 36% and 25% in 2027-28E (vs 8% and 10% in 
2025-26E).

Upside risks to our PO are (1) tighter ABF supply, (2) increasing adoption of SiP and AiP, 
(3) lower competition pressure and (4) slow development of alternative technology.

Downside risks are (1) greater ABF expansion from peers, (2) muted SiP/AiP adoption, (3) 
more severe competition, and (4) unfavorable technology development. 
NYPCB (NANYF) 
Our PO of NT$2,000 is based on 35x 2H27-1H28E P/E. We view this multiple as fair 
given ROE and operating margin expansion to 39% and 41% in 2027-28E (vs 13% and 
14% in 2025-26E).

Upside risks to our PO are (1) tighter ABF supply, (2) higher SiP/AiP adoption, and (3) 
less intensified competition pressure in PCB business.

Downside risks are (1) greater ABF expansion from peers, (2) muted SiP/AiP adoption, 
and (3) more severe competition in PCB business. 
Taiwan Union Technology Corporation (TWUNF) 
Our PO NT$2,600 is based on 27x 2H27-1H28E P/E, which is at the mid-to-high-end of 
the historical trading range of 8-31x during 2016-2026. In our view, the valuation 
multiple is justified, given operating margin and ROE expansion toward 26% and 63% in 
2026-28E (vs 13% and 16% in 2023-25) along with robust free-cash flow generation in 
over the same period.

Downside risks are 
1) Fluctuation in raw material prices, which could lead to a more volatile gross margin 
for TUC in the near term 
2) More intensified competition in CCL, which could pressure TUC's overall margin 
profile 
3) Adoption of new type of upstream material (e.g., quartz), which could bring business 
uncertainty for the names in CCL industry, including TUC 
4) Worse-than-expected execution by the company, which could cause market-share loss 
and/or decreased business traction with customers 
Unimicron (UMCRF) 
Our PO of NT$1,650 is based on 35x 2H27-1H28E, which is roughly 1 standard deviation 
above the historical average of 20x since 2017. In our view, the multiple is appropriate, 
as we expect Unimicron's earnings to be supported by its high-end technology offerings, 
where end market demand seems more resilient.

Upside risks are (1) tighter ABF supply, (2) greater PC and regular server demand, and (3) 
lower competition pressure.

Downside risks are (1) greater ABF expansion from peers, (2) lower ASP content increase 
at AI server, and (3) bigger drop in the demand of consumer electronic products.

FUNDAMENTAL EQUITY OPINION KEY: Opinions include a Volatility Risk Rating, an Investment Rating and an Income Rating. VOLATILITY RISK RATINGS, indicators 
of potential price fluctuation, are: A - Low, B - Medium and C - High. INVESTMENT RATINGS reflect the analyst’s assessment of both a stock’s absolute total return 
potential as well as its attractiveness for investment relative to other stocks within its Coverage Cluster (defined below). Our investment ratings are: 1 - Buy stocks 
are expected to have a total return of at least 10% and are the most attractive stocks in the coverage cluster; 2 - Neutral stocks are expected to remain flat or 
increase in value and are less attractive than Buy rated stocks and 3 - Underperform stocks are the least attractive stocks in a coverage cluster. An investment rating 
of 6 (No Rating) indicates that a stock is no longer trading on the basis of fundamentals. Analysts assign investment ratings considering, among other things, the 0-
12 month total return expectation for a stock and the firm’s guidelines for ratings dispersions (shown in the table below). The current price objective for a stock 
should be referenced to better understand the total return expectation at any given time. The price objective reflects the analyst’s view of the potential price 
appreciation (depreciation). 
Investment rating Total return expectation (within 12-month period of date of initial 
rating)

Buy ≥ 10% ≤ 70% 
Neutral ≥ 0% ≤ 30% 
Underperform N/A ≥ 20% 
R2Ratings dispersions may vary from time to time where BofA Global Research believes it better reflects the investment prospects of stocks in a Coverage Cluster. 
INCOME RATINGS, indicators of potential cash dividends, are: 7 - same/higher (dividend considered to be secure), 8 - same/lower (dividend not considered to be secure) 
and 9 - pays no cash dividend. Coverage Cluster is comprised of stocks covered by a single analyst or two or more analysts sharing a common industry, sector, 
region or other classification(s). A stock’s coverage cluster is included in the most recent BofA Global Research report referencing the stock.

精材(3374 TT)與 台 積 電 (2330 TT)簽 訂 先 進 封 裝 oS 產 能 外 包 協 議 ， 深 化 策 略 合 作 。 精
材 此 次 取 得 台 積 電 CoWoS 後段 oS 產 能 外 包 合 作 ， 對 長 期 業 務 結 構 升 級 具 正 面 意 義 ，
惟 股 價 於 公 告 前 已 連 續 6 個 交 易 日 上 漲 ， 累 計 漲 幅 約 40%，11/13/2026 收盤達 519 元
漲 停 ， 本 益 比 已 達 43 倍 ， 加 上 新 產 線 預 計 2029 年才開始第一期量產， 較 市 場 預 期
2027~2028 年量產晚， 短 期 獲 利 貢 獻 有 限 ， 且 仍 有 設 備 資 本 支 出 、 折 舊 及 CB 潛在稀
釋 風 險 ， 因 此 短 線 不 宜 追 價 。

研 究 員 ： 陳 俐 妍   
ly.c@capital.com.tw

台 積 電 擴 大 先 進 封 裝 外 包 布 局 ， 精 材 預 計 2029 年切入 CoWoS 後段 oS 製
程 ：  
精材(3374 TT) 於 11/13/2026 公 告 董 事 會 決 議 通 過 與 具 重 大 影 響 之 投 資 者 台
積電(2330 TT) 簽 訂 產 能 外 包 協 議 ， 契 約 期 間 自 2026/11/13 至 2033/12/31，
雙 方 將 針 對 先 進 封 裝 後 段 製 程 展 開 長 期 策 略 合 作。根 據 公 告 內 容，精 材 計 畫
進駐台積電中部科學園區廠房建置產線並負責生產營運，由台積電提供廠
房空間及先進封裝技術與製造能力支援，精材則自行投入生產設備及自動
物 料 搬 運 系 統 (AMHS)資 本 支 出，切 入 先 進 封 裝 供 應 鏈 中 的 oS(on-Substrate)
模 組 。 該 產 線 規 劃 於 2029 年 實 現 第 一 期 量 產 ， 雙 方 並 將 共 同 確 保 新 產 線 的
技 術 規 範、品 質 及 良 率 符 合 台 積 電 與 客 戶 要 求。此 外，精 材 董 事 會 同 步 通 過
發 行 新 台 幣 150 億 元 可 轉 換 公 司 債 (CB)， 以 支 應 初 期 產 能 建 置 需 求 。  
既 有 台 積 電 合 作 關 係 延 伸 至 先 進 封 裝 ， 有 助 精 材 拓 展 AI 及 HPC 業務：  
就 精 材 本 身 而 言，此 次 合 作 是 既 有 台 積 電 供 應 鏈 關 係 的 深 化。精 材 原 以 CIS
封 裝 及 晶 圓 級 封 裝 (WLCSP) 為 主 要 業 務，並 承 接 台 積 電 晶 圓 測 試 (CP)外 包 訂
單 ， 惟 既 有 測 試 機 台 於 08/2026 已 達 滿 載 ， 且 受 限 於 無 新 增 無 塵 室 空 間 ， 部
分 台 積 電 測 試 訂 單 轉 由 欣 銓 (3264 TT)承 接。此 次 透 過 進 駐 台 積 電 中 科 廠 區，
精材得以突破自身廠房空間限制，免除購地建廠所需的前置時間與土地成
本，並 利 用 台 積 電 既 有 廠 區 及 技 術 支 援，降 低 新 產 線 建 置 與 製 程 導 入 的 部 分
門 檻。由 於 台 積 電 目 前 持 有 精 材 約 41%股 權，雙 方 已 有 長 期 合 作 基 礎，此 次
進 一 步 由 晶 圓 測 試 及 既 有 封 裝 業 務 延 伸 至 CoWoS 後段 oS 製 程 ， 有 助 精 材
擴 大 與 台 積 電 的 合 作 範 圍，並 提 高 未 來 AI 及 HPC 相 關 業 務 的 營 收 占 比。不
過，CP 測 試 與 oS 屬 不 同 製 程，仍 須 觀 察 新 產 線 設 備 裝 機、製 程 驗 證 及 良 率
爬 升 進 度 。  
台 積 電 擴 大 先 進 封 裝 供 應 鏈 分 工 ， Interposer 與 oS 同 步 建 立 外 部 產 能 ：  
此 次 精 材 切 入 CoWoS 後段 oS 製 程 ， 應 放 在 台 積 電 持 續 擴 大 先 進 封 裝 供 應
鏈 分 工 的 整 體 策 略 下 觀 察 。 隨 AI 與 HPC 晶片朝 Chiplet、 HBM 及 異 質 整 合
發展，CoWoS 不 同 製 程 環 節 均 面 臨 產 能 擴 充 需 求 。 台 積 電 除 持 續 擴 充 自 身
先 進 封 裝 產 能 外，亦 逐 步 透 過 外 部 合 作 夥 伴 增 加 不 同 環 節 的 供 應 能 力，例 如
Interposer 方 面 與 GlobalFoundries 合 作 建 立 外 部 供 應 來 源，並 透 過 世 界 先 進
等 業 者 擴 充 相 關 產 能；後 段 先 進 封 裝 則 持 續 擴 大 與 OSAT 的 合 作，此 次 再 由
持股約 41%的 精 材 規 劃 於 台 積 電 中 科 廠 區 建 置 oS 產 線 ， 顯 示 台 積 電 正 逐 步
建 立 結 合 關 係 企 業 與 外 部 供 應 商 的 先 進 封 裝 供 應 鏈，以 提 高 產 能 配 置 彈 性。 
不 同 製 程 環 節 的 外 包 條 件 與 策 略 並 不 相 同 。 Interposer 外 包 主 要 在 於 增 加 供
應 來 源 及 產 能 彈 性；CoWoS 則 可 區 分 為 CoW 與 oS 兩 個 階 段，其 中 CoW 涉
及 晶 圓、矽 中 介 層 及 晶 片 間 的 高 精 度 整 合，製 程 複 雜 度 與 良 率 要 求 較 高，外

2 群益投顧 
包 仍 受 技 術 及 良 率 條 件 限 制；相 較 之 下，oS 主 要 負 責 將 完 成 的 CoW 模 組 接
合 至 基 板，隨 OSAT 業 者 製 程 經 驗 累 積 及 良 率 逐 步 成 熟，已 成 為 較 適 合 擴 大
委 外 的 環 節 ， 目 前 台 積 電 CoWoS 後段 oS 製 程 已 大 量 採 用 外 包 模 式 。 由 於
OSAT 的 先 進 封 裝 毛 利 率 通 常 低 於 台 積 電 整 體 製 造 業 務 ， 將 部 分 oS 產 能 交
由 外 部 業 者 承 接，亦 有 助 台 積 電 將 有 限 的 無 塵 室 空 間、設 備 及 工 程 資 源 優 先
配 置 於 技 術 門 檻 較 高 的 CoW 及 次 世 代 先 進 封 裝 製 程 。  
精材 2029 年 才 量 產 ， 2026～ 2028 年 既 有 OSAT 仍 持 續 受 惠 AI 封 裝 需 求 ：  
精 材 新 產 線 預 計 2029 年 才 開 始 第 一 期 量 產，因 此 在 2026～ 2028 年 間，並 不
會立即改變既有封測業者的供需格局。台積電雖可依製程整合需求向客戶
建 議 合 作 封 測 廠，但 客 戶 仍 可 依 自 身 需 求 直 接 與 封 測 業 者 洽 談 產 能 及 合 作，
因 此 先 進 封 裝 訂 單 並 非 完 全 由 台 積 電 單 方 面 分 配。在 AI/HPC 晶 片 持 續 推 動
先 進 封 裝 規 格 升 級 下，日 月 光 投 控、力 成 及 其 他 既 有 封 測 業 者 仍 可 持 續 受 惠
於新增外包需求，並透過製程改善、良率提升及設備效率優化增加有效產
出。 
至 2029 年 後 ， 精 材 oS 新 產 線 及 其 他 業 者 新 增 產 能 將 陸 續 開 出 ， 加 上 既 有
OSAT 持 續 透 過 良 率 改 善 增 加 有 效 產 能 ， 供 應 鏈 競 爭 可 能 逐 步 加 劇 。 因 此 ，
中 長 期 仍 須 觀 察 AI/HPC 需 求 成 長 能 否 持 續 吸 收 新 增 設 備 及 良 率 提 升 所 帶
來的有效供給，這將是影響先進封裝產業供需及各業者獲利表現的重要變
數。 
2029 年 後 新 增 有 效 產 能 可 能 增 加 ， 精 材 仍 面 臨 資 本 支 出 與 股 本 稀 釋 壓 力 ：  
雖然 AI/HPC 晶 片 持 續 朝 先 進 製 程、Chiplet 及 異 質 整 合 發 展，將 支 撐 先 進 封
裝 長 期 需 求 ， 但 目 前 CoWoS 供 不 應 求 ， 除 AI/HPC 需 求 快 速 成 長 、 晶 片 封
裝 面 積 擴 大 及 HBM 整 合 增 加 外 ， 製 程 複 雜 度 高 、 良 率 尚 待 提 升 ， 亦 可 能 限
制 有 效 產 能 釋 出。值 得 留 意 的 是，隨 製 程 技 術 持 續 改 進、設 備 調 校 及 量 產 經
驗 累 積，既 有 產 線 良 率 若 逐 步 提 升，即 使 未 新 增 設 備，也 可 能 增 加 有 效 產 出；
若 再 加 上 台 積 電 及 全 球 OSAT 業 者 持 續 擴 充 產 能 ， 未 來 供 給 增 幅 可 能 高 於
市 場 目 前 預 期 。 因 此 ， 現 階 段 CoWoS 供 不 應 求 未 必 能 長 期 延 續 ， 若 良 率 瓶
頸 逐 步 突 破，供 需 可 能 由 緊 俏 轉 向 平 衡，甚 至 出 現 供 給 較 為 充 裕 的 情 況，後
續 仍 須 觀 察 AI/HPC 需 求 成 長 能 否 吸 收 新 增 設 備 及 良 率 改 善 所 帶 來 的 有 效
供給。  
此 外 ， 精 材 須 自 行 負 擔 設 備 及 AMHS 資 本 支 出 ， 設 備 投 資 至 新 產 線 營 收 貢
獻 仍 有 時 間 落 差，設 備 陸 續 投 入 使 用 後 亦 將 增 加 折 舊 費 用，若 初 期 產 能 利 用
率 及 良 率 爬 升 不 如 預 期，可 能 對 毛 利 率 及 營 業 利 益 形 成 壓 力。資 金 方 面，精
材截至 11/2026 股 本 為 27.14 億 元 ， 約 當 2.714 億 股 ， 預 計 發 行 150 億元
CB， 目 前 尚 未 確 定 轉 換 價 格 ； 若 以 11/13/2026 收 盤 價 519 元 作 為 假 設 轉 換
價格，並以全數轉換估算，新增股數約 2,890 萬 股 ， 相 較 目 前 股 數 增 加 約
10.65%， 轉 換 後 原 股 東 持 股 比 例 將 稀 釋 約 9.63%。 惟 此 為 假 設 情 境 ， 實 際 稀
釋 程 度 仍 須 視 最 終 發 行 金 額、轉 換 價 格 及 轉 換 條 件 而 定。因 此，此 次 合 作 雖
有助精材長期業務結構升級，但仍須考量資本支出回收效益、折舊吸收能
力、CB 潛 在 稀 釋 及 未 來 產 業 供 需 變 化 ， 不 宜 直 接 以 目 前 CoWoS 供 不 應 求
的 狀 況 外 推 未 來 獲 利 。

精 材 短 線 評 價 已 反 映 部 分 成 長 預 期 ， 封 測 股 則 可 留 意 修 正 後 的 布 局 機 會 ：  
精 材 此 次 取 得 台 積 電 CoWoS 後段 oS 產 能 外 包 合 作 ， 對 長 期 業 務 結 構 升 級
具 正 面 意 義 ， 惟 股 價 於 公 告 前 已 連 續 6 個 交 易 日 上 漲 ， 累 計 漲 幅 約 40%，

潛 在 稀 釋 風 險 ， 因 此 短 線 不 宜 追 價 。

及力成(6239 TT) 的 中 短 期 投 資 看 法 。 2026 年至 2028 年 精 材 新 產 線 尚 未 量
產，既 有 封 測 廠 仍 可 持 續 承 接 AI 及 HPC 先 進 封 裝 與 測 試 外 溢 訂 單，並 透 過
產 線 效 率 及 良 率 改 善 提 升 有 效 產 出，中 短 期 應 以 訂 單 能 見 度、產 能 利 用 率 及
獲 利 表 現 作 為 主 要 觀 察 重 點。4Q26 以 來，部 分 封 測 股 已 因 市 場 對 AI 資 本 支
出 及 投 資 效 益 的 疑 慮 而 大 幅 修 正，若 後 續 訂 單 及 營 運 展 望 未 明 顯 轉 弱，評 價
修正反而可能提供布局機會，可持續留意受惠先進封裝需求的日月光投控
(3711 TT) 、力成(6239 TT)， 以 及 承 接 晶 圓 測 試 外 溢 需 求 的 欣 銓 (3264 TT)。
至 2029 年 後 ， 隨 精 材 及 其 他 業 者 新 增 產 能 陸 續 開 出 ， 加 上 既 有 產 線 良 率 提
升 帶 動 有 效 供 給 增 加 ， 則 須 留 意 供 需 變 化 及 競 爭 加 劇 風 險 。

產業速報  
                                        2026 年 11 月 16 日

4 群益投顧 
  【 投 資 評 等 說 明 】  
  評等 定義 
  強力買進(Strong Buy)  首 次 評 等 潛 在 上 漲 空 間 ≥ 35%  
  買進(Buy)  15%≤首 次 評 等 潛 在 上 漲 空 間 <35%  
  區間操作(Trading Buy) 5%≤首 次 評 等 潛 在 上 漲 空 間 <15%

中立(Neutral)  
無 法 由 基 本 面 給 予 投 資 評 等  
預 期 近 期 股 價 將 處 於 盤 整  
建 議 降 低 持 股

【 投 資 評 等 調 整 機 制 】  
  投 資 評 等 係「 首 次 」給 予 特 定 個 股 投 資 評 等 時，其 前 一 日 收 盤 價 相 較 3 個月
目 標 價 之 潛 在 上 漲 空 間 計 算 而 得 。 個 股 投 資 評 等 分 為 四 個 等 級 ， 定 義 如 上 。
爾 後 的 投 資 評 等 係 依 循 「 首 次 評 等 」 ， 直 到 停 止 推 薦 。   
「 強 力 買 進 」、「 買 進 」 及 「 區 間 操 作 」 均有 upside 目 標 價 。 差 別 僅 在 於 ，
首 次 評 等 時 潛 在 報 酬 率 不 同 。 「 中 立 」 則 無 目 標 價 。  
一 旦 我 們 給 予 特 定 個 股「 強 力 買 進 」、「 買 進 」或「 區 間 操 作 」之 投 資 評 等，
就 是 責 任 的 開 始，爾 後 將 透 過 各 式 研 究 報 告 作 定 期 性、持 續 性 追 蹤 基 本 面 及
股 價 變 化 ， 直 到 停 止 推 薦 。  
停 止 推 薦 情 境 ：  
1. 達 目 標 價 。  
2. 雖 未 達 原 訂 目 標 價 ， 但 檢 視 基 本 面 、 訊 息 面 、 籌 碼 面 等 多 方 訊 息 ， 研
判 股 價 上 漲 空 間 已 然 有 限 ， 將 適 時 出 具 降 評 報 告 。  
3. 推 薦 後 股 價 不 漲 反 跌 ， 亦 將 出 具 降 評 報 告 。

【免責聲明】 
  本 研 究 報 告 僅 提 供 予 特 定 人 之 客 戶 作 為 參 考 資 料「 非 經 同 意 不 得 轉 載 」。我
們 並 不 確 保 此 資 訊 的 完 整 性 與 正 確 性，投 資 人 應 了 解，報 告 中 有 關 未 來 預 測
之 陳 述 可 能 不 會 實 現，因 而 不 應 被 依 賴。而 且 此 報 告 並 非 根 據 特 定 投 資 目 的
或 依 預 定 對 象 之 財 務 狀 況 所 撰 寫 出 來 的，因 此，此 研 究 報 告 的 目 的，既 非 對
投 資 人 於 買 賣 證 券、選 擇 權、期 貨 或 其 他 證 券 相 關 之 衍 生 性 商 品 提 供 詢 價 服
務，亦 非 作 為 進 行 交 易 的 要 約。投 資 人 應 注 意 到 相 關 證 券 之 價 值 及 收 益，可
能會有無預警地上升或下降，產生投資回報金額可能比原始投資來得少的
情形。

삼성전자 
(005930) 
 결국 제자리를 찾아갈 것 
류형근

hyungkeun.ryu@daishin.com

투자의견 BUY  
매수, 유지 
6개월 목표주가 500,000 
유지  
현재주가 
(26.10.29) 
231,000 
반도체업종

◼ 3분기 실적발표에 대한 시장의 반응은 다소 냉소적. 
◼ 주주환원은 시점의 문제일 뿐. 강화 방향성은 여전히 유효. 
◼ 메모리반도체 이익의 폭증이 가격의 매력을 유발. 비중확대 전략 유효.

매력적인 가격, 변하지 않은 주주환원 정책의 방향성 
목표주가와 매수의견 유지. 메모리반도체 주도 폭발적 이익 상승이 가격 (주
가)의 매력을 지속 유발하고 있고, 주주환원 정책의 강화 방향성에도 변한 것
은 없다는 판단. 절대적으로 싼 주가를 비중 확대의 기회로 삼을 필요. 
1. 주주환원, 시점의 문제 
금번 실적발표에 대한 시장의 반응은 다소 냉소적. 대외 불확실성이 큰 만큼, 
시장은 자사주 매입/소각과 같은 즉각적 주가 부양 정책의 발표를 기대.  
당사는 삼성전자의 주주환원이 “시점의 문제”라 생각. 총 주주환원의 규모도, 
차기 (2027-2029년) 주주환원 정책의 강화 방향에도 변한 것은 없다는 판
단. 시장이 기대하는 자사주 매입/소각은 우선주 중심으로 시행될 가능성이 
높으며, 시행 시, 그 규모는 25조원 내외일 것 (DPS 12,567원)으로 전망. 
2. 메모리반도체, 더 올라갈 것 
보수적 환율 전망 (2027년 평균 환율 1,300원 적용)에도, 2027년 메모리반도
체 영업이익은 608조원에 달할 것. 이익 전망의 상향 여력 또한 유효. 
1) 최대 생산, 최대 판매 
삼성전자는 금일 4Q26 DRAM 판매량 가이던스로 전분기 대비 10% 중후반 
성장 (q-q +18%로 추정)을 제시. 일부 투자자들은 높아진 기저 부담으로 
2027년 판매 성장이 제한될 것을 우려. 당사는 높아진 기저에도 2027년 
DRAM 판매가 전년대비 20% 상승할 것으로 전망. 
- 극단적 수급 불균형 속, 삼성전자는 “최대 생산, 최대 판매” 정책을 시행 
중. 삼성전자만의 강력한 Execution이 20% 대 성장을 유지시킬 원동력. 
- DRAM 1c 수율의 조기 개선 속, 범용 DRAM 양산에 DRAM 1c Capa가 일
부 투입될 것으로 예상. Bit 성장을 높일 수 있는 변화가 될 것. 
2) 열려 있는 가격의 상방 
1Q27 모바일 DRAM에서 Surprise가 발생할 것이라는 의견을 유지 (전분기 
대비 30% 이상 상승). 장기계약이 체결된 CSP향으로도 가격의 추가 상승 여
력이 존재한다는 점을 상기할 필요. CSP의 경우, 당장의 비용 부담보다 AI 시
장 선점을 우선시. 장기계약으로 형성된 가격 Band의 천장 (Ceiling) 또한 시
장 우려 대비 낮지 않음을 감안할 필요. 2027년 범용 DRAM ASP는 전년대비 
40% 중후반까지 상승할 가능성 (현 전망: 전년대비 34% 상승).  
3) HBM, 제품 경쟁력과 점유율의 동반 성장  
최근 삼성전자향으로 HBM4/HBM4e 추가 공급 요청이 쇄도하고 있는 것으
로 추정. 일부 고객은 연내 추가 공급 시 2027년 계약 가격을 선제 지급할 
의사를 표출. 제품 경쟁력이 회복되었음을 보여주는 증거라 생각. 
제품 경쟁력의 회복과 최대 Capa의 이점 속, 2027년은 HBM 점유율 1위를 
확보하는 기념비적 해가 될 것으로 전망 (매출액 기준 점유율 38% 예상).

ECONOMIC INSIGHT
• Unemployment and output both show a common pattern
of downturns being short, sharp and irregular, while the
subsequent recoveries and expansions are longer and
slower. The eminent economist Milton Friedman likened
this pattern to plucking on a string. The causes are unclear,

though there are some plausible candidates.
• If we take the “plucking” pattern seriously, there are clear
policy implications. Standard models used by central banks
do not allow for this pattern and therefore systematically
overstate how often capacity is tight. And when a
downturn does occur, the “plucking” view argues for
forceful stabilisation policy.
• We believe the current slow upward grind in
unemployment in Australia is a different phenomenon

and expect slow growth rather than a sharp downturn. If
people start thinking current inflation pressures demand a
1980s-style “Volcker disinflation”, though, it could just end
up plucking the economy.
Look at a graph of the unemployment rate for almost any
western economy, and you will notice a few things. The
big increases are sharp and sudden, while the subsequent
recoveries are slow and last longer. The timing of those upward
spikes is irregular, with no obvious pattern. The height of the

spike in unemployment is a good predictor of how far the rate
declines in the recovery that follows. By contrast, neither the
length of an expansion phase of declining unemployment, nor
the size of the decline predict the size of the next downturn.
This pattern is a far cry from the notion of a business
cycle, where output and unemployment oscillate round
some average or trend. There is something different about
downturns. They are not just like expansions but a bit slower.

The eminent economist Milton Friedman described this as a
“plucking” model. The economy, he suggested, was a bit like a
guitar string. Most of the time it stayed close to the fretboard.
There is a physical maximum amount of production possible
at any one time. Occasionally, though, the economy could be
plucked away from that maximum, with output falling and
unemployment rising sharply.
Remarkably, the mainstream macroeconomic literature that
central banks rely on has engaged little with this feature of the

data. Forecasting models used by the RBA and its peers do not
produce this pattern. Some authors have looked at “plucking”-
type models, though. Their analysis confirmed that downturns
predict the size of the following expansion, but the reverse
Is the economy ‘plucked’?
Luci Ellis
Chief Economist, Westpac Group
is not true. Expansions do not “die of old age” by building up
imbalances. Australia went for 30 years without a proper
downturn: it turns out that this is entirely possible provided

nothing bad enough happens.
These authors further argue that the maximum production level
during expansions is buffeted by small shocks, some temporary,
others positive and permanent – think new technologies and
ways of doing things, or a policy change that encourages
workforce participation. Downturns, by contrast, are triggered
by large shocks that are usually temporary and arrive at irregular
intervals. Some argue that it is best to think of economies as

having three phases: normal expansions, which can continue
for long periods; shorter-lived downturns; and subsequent
recovery phases where growth can outstrip normal rates and
unemployment declines, but at a slower pace than it rose.
Some downturns do not end in full recovery, however. This
observation links to another strand of research showing that
the output losses associated with financial crises and wars are
not fully recovered. The experience after the Global Financial

Crisis shows this pattern in a number of advanced economies.
Standard models would interpret this as slower growth in
potential output, perhaps feeding the Australian tradition of
hand-wringing about insufficient productivity or reform.
While the pattern in the data is clear, the causes of “plucking”
are far from settled, with some authors seeing boom–bust
dynamics as inherent in the economic system rather than
driven by shocks. Some recent papers – including preliminary

RBA work – look to certain frictions in the labour market
for an explanation. These include search frictions, which
arise because workers and firms sometimes require time to
find a good match with each other. Another at least partial
explanation is that workers resist absolute wage cuts but
accept falls in purchasing power when dollar wages are
constant in the face of positive inflation.
An objection to this line of research is the finding by some BIS

researchers that the plucking pattern is clearer in countries
with more flexible labour markets – mostly English-speaking
ones. The literature focusing on labour market frictions also
does not explain where the originating pluck comes from,
“Downturns are usually short, sharp and
arrive at irregular intervals; expansions are
longer and slower."

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
only why the data have the dynamics they do when those
shocks occur. Something must be causing the “pluck” shock,
if indeed it is a shock. The BIS paper suggests one possibility:
the authors find that output in the manufacturing and trade
sectors shows a clearer pluck pattern than construction.

Construction downturns tend to linger, perhaps because of
their connections to indebtedness and financial crises.
If we take seriously the patterns in the data showing
asymmetric downturns and the plucking pattern Friedman
described, there are clear policy implications. Currently, the
models underlying central bank forecasts are symmetric by
construction. They therefore systematically understate spare
capacity during expansions. They will find a positive output

gap ahead of a downturn, even when there was no evidence of
overheating at the time. This oversight risks policy mistakes.
The asymmetric nature of downturns also means that the
benefits of macroeconomic stabilisation policy are mostly
about cushioning downturns and hastening recoveries. In
normal times, the benefits will be small.
We also note that the RBA paper mentioned above hypothesised
a short-run NAIRU and a long-run NAIRU that are both pushed

around by actual unemployment in a path-dependent pattern.
This is an argument for “holding on to the employment gains”
that may have been missed by people whose mental model of
the economy is fully symmetric, without any plucking.
The plucking model started from empirical observations
about large downturns. The kind of slow grind upwards in
unemployment seen in Australia in the 2010s and in the past
couple of years does not fit this pattern. It is a qualitatively

different phenomenon requiring other explanations, including
perhaps the path-dependent drag upwards of the feasible
unemployment rate by the actual unemployment rate.
It is these kinds of considerations that underlie our current
forecast for slow growth and not a downturn, even though this
week’s labour market data were concerning. A further 25 basis
points or so of rate increases is unlikely to be the thing that
causes a severe downturn. Typically, downturns are not just

slower expansions but are qualitatively different phases that
require a trigger.
The one thing that makes me nervous is that the plucking
literature finds that every downturn is different. The famous
opening line to Tolstoy’s Anna Karenina, “Happy families are
all alike; every unhappy family is unhappy in its own way,”
would seem to apply to economies as well. And in at least one
case, a forceful tightening of monetary policy was the trigger
for a downturn. The so-called “Volcker disinflation” brought

US inflation under control in the early 1980s. That episode is
widely regarded as a success, despite the output costs. In the
current circumstances, some policymakers and commentators
might be tempted to believe that the same medicine is needed;
nobody wants inflation expectations to become entrenched,
after all. However, as RBA Board member Ian Ross has noted,
the institutions of the 1970s that produced wage–price spirals
are no longer operative. So there is no guarantee that a similar

approach nowadays would be necessary or as effective.
Watch out then, if the discourse turns to arguments that
countries “need a recession” to get inflation under control. A
Volcker-style policy could just end up plucking the economy.
Forecast changes
• We have upgraded our WPI forecast following this week's data
release, with our year-end forecast now 3.4%yr.
• We have also upgraded our near-term unemployment rate,
with a year-end forecast of 4.9%.

THE WEEK THAT WAS
Cliff Notes: risks mount
risks are skewed to the upside. FOMC members who spoke
throughout the week also kept the focus on price risks, and called
for the FOMC to act swiftly. Governor Waller again proposed a
50bp hike in December; others seemingly favour a 25bp hike at

the December and January meetings, as Westpac forecasts, and
continuing to tighten further in 2027 if necessary.
Very clearly, the labour market and activity growth are not
of concern for US policy makers. Retail sales gave further
justification for this view this week, the control group
surprising to the upside with a 0.6% gain in October after
a material upward revision to September (0.5%). That said,
housing is under considerable pressure, pending home sales

falling 17.1% over the past year, and housing starts back at
2019 levels despite a 9.9% rebound in October. Persistent
weakness in this sector will, in time, have implications for both
consumption and investment.
China’s October data round was the other key international
development of the week. Again, it provided a call for pro-
active stimulus in scale. In the month, annual retail sales
growth almost stalled at 0.4%yr and the decline in fixed
asset investment broadened outside property, with capital

expenditure now down 8.1% across the economy year-to-
date. Industrial production again highlighted that its output to
satiate global demand which is holding up aggregate growth;
though, even here, momentum softened in October, annual
growth in production slowing from 5.5%yr to 4.8%yr. It is not
only activity that remains under pressure, but also wealth,
with new / existing home prices 0.15% / 0.24% lower in the
month. As we continue to emphasise, to prove effective,

stimulus needs to affect change in both activity and wealth
and do so in a sustainable manner.
Australia’s labour market disappointed again in October,
employment falling 43.5k following September’s 36.4k
decline. On a three-month average basis, job gains over the
year have now slowed to 1.1%yr, materially below current
growth in the working-age population, 1.7%yr. As a result, the
unemployment rate rose to a new post-pandemic high of 4.9%
despite the participation rate falling another 0.2ppts to 66.6%.

While employment growth’s descent initially reflected softer
momentum in hiring, now we are seeing evidence of a material
increase in redundancies. Most affected workers look to have
remained in employment to date, but persistent job shedding
raises the risk of a faster and more persistent uptrend in the
unemployment rate, potentially above our baseline peak
forecast of 5.2% (at mid-2027) and most certainly before the
late-2028 peak of 5.1% the RBA expects.

As such, the robust 1.1% (3.5%yr) rise in the Q3 Wage Price
Index warrants close scrutiny. The Q3 increase was skewed
towards those on awards and enterprise arrangements – pay
outcomes directly tied to the Fair Work Commission’s decision
and collective bargaining. Individual arrangements meanwhile,
the most responsive to current and expected labour market
conditions, accounted for their smallest share of a Q3 wage
increase since 2019. Business survey detail also suggests labour

cost growth will normalise quickly after the award increase.
Deteriorating economic conditions are also increasingly evident
in housing. The Westpac Housing Sentiment Index fell 2.2%
to 83.2 in November, among the weakest readings since 2005.
Interestingly, there remains considerable latent demand: almost
13% of consumers intend to purchase a home over the next
year, and relatively few plan to sell. For now, though, weak
sentiment, high interest rates and expectations of further price

declines are keeping would-be buyers on the sidelines. These
factors also pose risks to the broader growth story.
In the US, abstracting from the Anthropic IPO, the outlook for
inflation remained the critical theme. The October FOMC minutes
highlighted that inflation is currently being supported by tariffs
and energy prices as well as the AI buildout, with "several
[members] judg[ing] that price pressures were increasingly
extending beyond industries directly affected by data center

construction”. Participants "assessed that medium- and longer-
term inflation expectations continued to be consistent with the
Committee's 2 percent objective", although persistent above-
target inflation is a threat to the status quo, and perceived
Elliot Clarke, Head of International Economics
Ryan Wells, Economist
Illiana Jain, Economist

consecutive shock in October, pushing annual
employment growth well below working-age

population growth."

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
FOCUS ON NEW ZEALAND
Sunny side up
This week we published our final Economic Overview
for 2026. Titled “Sunny side up”, the report shows that
New Zealand’s economic recovery is continuing. Activity
expanded by 2.6% in the year to June, and recent indicators

point to further moderate growth. Overall, GDP is forecast
to grow by 2.8% in both 2027 and 2028. But this remains a
two-speed recovery: export-facing industries and regional
centres are enjoying stronger incomes, while households
and businesses tied to the major metropolitan centres are
still contending with high inflation, a soft labour market and
a subdued housing market.
The global backdrop remains broadly supportive. On an export-
weighted basis, New Zealand’s trading partners are forecast

to grow by 3.1% in 2026, 2.9% in 2027 and 2.8% in 2028.
Stronger growth in the United States and East Asia outside
China, helped by the continuing artificial intelligence investment
buildout, is offsetting some of the drag from higher energy
prices. The central assumption underpinning these forecasts is
that a Middle East agreement eventually restores safe passage
through the Strait of Hormuz, allowing Brent crude to fall
towards US$77 a barrel in late 2027 and US$71 in late 2028.

Against that backdrop, the external sector is the clear bright
spot. Merchandise export volumes are estimated to have risen
by more than 4% this year, with dairy, meat and horticulture
joined by stronger output of manufactured metals, machinery
and equipment. Favourable growing conditions, high world
prices and a low New Zealand dollar have driven this growth.
Services exports are performing even more strongly and
are on track to grow by around 9% this year. Foreign visitor

arrivals continue to recover strongly, with Australians and
Chinese accounting for about two-thirds of the increase
seen this year. In 12-month rolling terms, visitor numbers
are expected to soon move above their 2019 peak, while
other services exports will likely continue their longer-run
outperformance.
Agriculture is expected to remain a major source of strength.
Farmgate returns are expected to stay elevated despite high
energy and shipping costs and an expected modest appreciation

in the New Zealand dollar. Meat export prices should remain
high in real terms over the next couple of years, with beef
supported by firm US demand. Kiwifruit and apple prices are
also expected to remain strong, while log prices should be
broadly stable to slightly firmer in 2027. Dairy prices – at least
powders – appear to have received an additional lift from El
Niño-related drought risk and strong global demand for protein.
We have raised our forecast milk price for the current season

by 50 cents to $10.25 per kilogram of milk solids – still ahead
of Fonterra’s own forecast, which was revised up to a $10.10
midpoint this week. We have also lifted our 2027/28 season
forecast to $9.75, reflecting a more gradual expected rise in the
New Zealand dollar than foreseen previously.
At this point, the strength offshore has not yet translated
into an equally convincing recovery for households. Average
household earnings grew only 2.9% in the year to June, while

high inflation pushed real wage growth into negative territory.
Real per capita household spending was flat over the year, and
weak house prices have limited growth in household wealth.
Spending should improve gradually during 2027 as export
income circulates through the economy, employment recovers
and inflation eases. Even so, the strongest gains are likely to
remain in regions directly exposed to agriculture and tourism.
The labour market has been unusually slow to respond to

improving GDP. Unemployment has climbed to an 11-year high
of 5.7%, and employment growth has failed to keep pace with
population growth. The pattern is best described as “low-
fire, low-hire”: firms avoided the scale of layoffs that might
normally accompany a downturn but may now have enough
staff to accommodate the early stages of a recovery without
immediately stepping up recruitment. Employment appears
to have begun turning higher and hiring intentions have

recovered, but unemployment is unlikely to decline materially
until next year. We expect it to ease to 5.1% by the end of
2027 and 4.5% in 2028.
Housing remains a restraint. National house prices have fallen
about 1.7% this year, sales have weakened and properties are
taking longer to sell. Adjusted for inflation, house prices are on
course for a fourth consecutive annual decline, and we expect
them to fall by around 33% between the 2021 peak and the

end of 2027. That adjustment is improving affordability and
supporting first-home buyers, but it also encourages existing
owners to save rather than spend. As with other economic
indicators, regional differences remain pronounced, with areas
benefiting from agricultural and tourism income outperforming
the major centres.
Residential construction should nevertheless rise during
2027, supported by a pipeline of more than 40,000 consented
homes, much of it medium-density development in Auckland

and Canterbury. The durability of that upswing is uncertain,
however, because financing costs, building costs and weak
house prices remain significant obstacles. Commercial
construction is closer to finding a floor, although office and
retail development is likely to stay subdued for a while given
elevated vacancy rates.
Darren Gibbs
Senior Economist

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
Business investment has been more resilient. Capital equipment
spending rose strongly in the June quarter, and imports point to
further gains despite higher freight costs and a weaker currency.
Confidence rebounded after the initial shock from the Middle

East conflict and has held up despite renewed oil price pressure.
Investment will remain uneven, but cash-rich agricultural
businesses are well placed, and computing equipment and
software are contributing to growth.
Inflation is the main complication. Consumer prices rose 3.7%
in the year to September and annual inflation is expected to
reach 4.3% in December. Higher global oil prices have played
an important role, but the problem is broader than fuel. Core

inflation measures remain around 3%, operating costs are
elevated and firms’ pricing intentions have strengthened.
Headline inflation should fall as energy prices ease and could
temporarily dip below 2% in 2028, but underlying inflation is
expected to decline much more slowly.
That persistence means the RBNZ will likely need to tighten
policy by more than it currently forecasts. The OCR is expected
to reach 3% by year-end, but we think the neutral rate may

be closer to 3.75%, in contrast to the RBNZ’s 3% to 3.5%
estimate. As growth strengthens and core inflation proves
sticky, we expect the OCR to peak at 4% in September 2027
and remain there for about a year. Medium- and long-term
interest rates are therefore likely to settle above the unusually
low levels seen in the decade before the pandemic.
Finally, fiscal conditions should improve as stronger nominal
activity and above-target inflation lift tax revenue. However,

spending pressures and higher debt-servicing costs will be
difficult to avoid. We expect the operating balance before
gains and losses, excluding ACC (OBEGALx), to return to
surplus in 2028/29, while net core Crown debt peaks near
43.7% of GDP in 2027/28. The unresolved election adds
uncertainty, however; the forecasts assume no major near-
term change in policy direction.
Turning to this week’s key economic news, RBNZ Governor
Anna Breman and fellow MPC member Prasanna Gai spoke

to audiences in New Zealand and Australia respectively. We
thought that Breman’s comments were reasonably balanced.
On the hawkish side, Breman noted that some indicators of
economic activity had been stronger than expected. Perhaps
of greater note, she also said that this month’s partial October
CPI data (discussed below) indicated that indirect impacts
from the fuel price shock might be growing following a more
muted than expected impact in the September quarter. On

the dovish side, she noted subdued domestic demand, which
she said was making it difficult for firms to pass on higher
costs. She also referred to weak labour market data (including
weak wage growth) and the stability seen in medium-term
inflation expectations (also discussed below). Following the
October review Breman had commented that the chances of a
December rate hike were “high”.
In our view, Gai’s commentary leaned more on the dovish
side. He reiterated his concerns about the implications for NZ

of a bursting of the AI bubble and described recent inflation
and inflation expectations data as “quiescent”. In response to
a question, he indicated that he considered that news since
the October meeting had validated his decision to vote for a
pause at that meeting. The general tone of Gai’s commentary
suggested it would not be a surprise if he voted to extend the
pause at next month’s meeting. Even if he were to vote for
a hike, it seems likely that Gai would regard this as simply a

tentative step towards the roughly 3.25% neutral rate that the
Bank projected in the September Monetary Policy Statement.
On the data front, the partial CPI data for October pointed
to weaker than expected food price inflation but firmer than
expected increases in travel costs. The latter was probably
responsible for Breman’s comments above, but given the
month-to-month volatility of this data we would be hesitant
to read too much into one month’s outcome. These opposing

impacts netted out and so the outcome was consistent
with our forecast for a lift in annual inflation to 4.3% in the
December quarter. Meanwhile, the RBNZ’s long-running
survey of professional forecasters reported a lift in year-ahead
inflation expectations. However, encouragingly, the closely
watched 2-year-ahead measure was little changed at 2.36%.
Moreover, 5-year-ahead and 10-year-ahead expectations –
which had been singled out for special attention by RBNZ

Chief Economist Conway in a recent speech – fell to 2.17%
and 2.13% respectively. In both cases, these were the
lowest readings in almost two years. Finally, encouragingly,
the BusinessNZ services PMI continued its gradual trend
improvement in October, rising to a 3-year high of 51.7.

We expect this positive momentum to have carried into Q3 
2026, with further solid gains in residential building, private 
non-residential building and private engineering construction, 
particularly electricity-related projects. Public infrastructure 
investment will offset some of this strength, with the drag that 
began last year continuing as major projects reach completion.
AUS: October CPI (%mth)
November 25, Last: 0.8%, Westpac f/c: 0.4%  
Mkt f/c: 0.4%, Range : 0.3 to 0.6
We expect the October CPI to rise 0.41%mth with the annual 
pace lifting from 4.3%yr to 4.8%yr, the fastest pace in the 
short history. Westpac expects this to be the peak in current 
acceleration in annual inflation. Our seasonally adjusted CPI 
estimate lifts 0.7%mth, a small step up from the 0.6% increase 
in September, highighting the seasonal softness in the month. 
Transport makes the largest contribution (+0.35ppts), almost 
entirely due to auto fuel, with smaller contributions from 
clothing & footwear (+0.06ppts), household goods & services 
(+0.05ppts) and meals out & takeaway (+0.04ppts). The 
main offsets are recreation & culture (–0.18ppts) with fruit & 
vegetables, utilities and others providing marginal offsets.
For more details please see “October CPI Preview“.  
AUS: October Trimmed Mean (%mth)
November 25, Last: 0.2%, Westpac f/c: 0.4% 
Mkt f/c: 0.4%, Range : 0.3 to 0.5
Our October Trimmed Mean estimate is 0.42%mth, a step up from 
0.3% in September, taking the annual pace to 3.7%yr from 3.6%yr 
and lifting the six month annualised pace to 4.2%yr, from 4.0%yr, 
which is also the fastest pace in the short history of the series. 
Rents and dwellings continue to underpin the TM estimates. 
We see risks as slightly tilted to the upside. Our estimates 
allow for a greater degree of price resetting in areas such as 
meals out & takeaway and personal and household services, 
but this adjustment proves broader than our assumptions. 
Fuel levy surcharges are another source of upside risk, 
including increases by Woolworths and Australia Post. The 
key downside risk remains housing, particularly new dwelling 
prices, which surprised to the downside in recent months.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
NZ: Q3 retail spending (%qtr)
Nov 23, Last: -0.5, Westpac: 1.4
The June quarter saw a larger than expected 0.5% fall in
retail spending, with high fuel prices squeezing households’
spending power. However, even with the drag from high
fuel prices, core spending (excl. fuels and vehicles) remained

resilient, rising by 0.7%. With fuel prices dropping through
much of the September quarter, we expect overall spending
levels will lift by 1.4%. Importantly, with lower fuel prices
putting money back into households’ pockets, that’s likely to
be underpinned by firm gains in core spending categories.
AUS: Q3 Private CAPEX (%qtr)
Nov 26, Last: –3.6%, WBC f/c: 10.0%
Mkt f/c: 4.5%, Range: 2.0% to 10.0%
Private capex declined 3.6%qtr in Q2 2026 as data centre-

related machinery and equipment investment pulled back
sharply. With nominal ADP equipment imports surging
83%qtr to another record high, we expect a sizeable rebound
in the Q3 figures. Buildings and structures should also find
support as the broader strength in private non-residential
approvals through the first half of 2026 works through the
pipeline. Overall, we expect private capex to grow a strong
10.0%qtr in Q3, with data centres again leading the charge.

Est 3 for 2026/27 capex plans, after our adjustment, is
consistent with impressive nominal growth of 17.9%yr and
real growth of 15.3%yr. We expect Est 4 unadjusted plans for
2026/27 to come in at $221bn, a 15.5% increase on a year ago,
indicative of the scale of investment yet to occur.

MACRO STRATEGY
Consumers Feel One Way, but Spend Another
Kirsten Cabacungan, Vice President and Senior Investment Strategist
Americans have spent much of the past several years telling surveys they are cautious
about the economy. Yet they continue to spend as though they are not. The University
of Michigan’s Consumer Sentiment Index remains near multidecade lows, while the
Conference Board’s Index of Consumer Confidence has also been in a yearslong downtrend.

These surveys reflect a list of persistent concerns from elevated food and gas prices to
geopolitical uncertainty and labor market anxiety.
Even so, consumer spending has remained remarkably resilient. The final estimate of Q2
real consumer spending, as measured by PCE, was revised up to an annualized rate of
3.8%, well above the 2.3% 20-year average. More recent data suggest that momentum
has continued. According to Bank of America Institute, the latest monthly total credit and

debit card spending data per household rose 6.2% year-over-year (YoY) in September, and
spending excluding gasoline was up 5.1% YoY, both the third strongest growth rates in the
past four years.1
The persistence of consumer spending is particularly notable given that temporary cyclical
boosts like larger tax refunds are starting to fade. That raises an important question: What is
actually funding strong consumption today? Recent data suggest the drivers are becoming

increasingly more diversified, with broadening labor market trends, healthy consumer
finances and elevated wealth effects all reinforcing one another and underpinning consumers.
First, the labor market continues to provide an important foundation for consumer spending,
and recent trends suggest wage growth is supporting a broader set of households than in
recent years. Jobs growth has slowed from the exceptionally strong pace seen following the
pandemic, but broader indicators suggest labor market conditions remain relatively stable.

Although the September jobs report came in softer than expected, the three- and six-month
rolling averages indicate that the trend in jobs growth likely bottomed out last year and has
gradually improved since (Exhibit 1A). Meanwhile, the unemployment rate remains low by
historical standards and layoffs remain contained. And while wage growth has moderated
and recently trailed headline inflation (Exhibit 1B), cumulative income gains over the past
several years have likely helped many households absorb higher prices better.

An important development though has been improving wage growth trends among lower-
income households, where spending tends to be most sensitive to changes in wages. Bank
of America Institute consumer deposit account data show after-tax wage growth for lower-
income households outpaced higher-income households for a second consecutive month
in August, rising 4.7% YoY compared with 3.5% for higher-income households. Importantly,
this improvement appears to be supporting discretionary spending, not just spending

on necessities. Lower-income discretionary spending rose 5.7% YoY in August, nearly
matching the 5.9% pace for higher-income households. In other words, the narrowing gap
in wage and spending growth across income cohorts suggests consumer resilience looks
to be becoming less dependent on affluent households alone, creating a broader and more
durable foundation for consumption.
Second, household finances remain in relatively good shape, providing consumers with the

flexibility to continue spending. The ratio of household debt to disposable personal income
remains near its lowest level since the late 1990s, excluding the temporary distortions
caused by pandemic-related income support in 2020 and 2021 (Exhibit 1C). Recent
revisions to the Bureau of Economic Analysis national income accounts also suggest
consumers have a larger financial buffer than previous data suggested. Personal saving
estimates over the past year have been revised higher by $320 billion, or 39%, and the

revised savings rate remains above 4% after previous estimates showed it fell to 3% during
the summer.2 Concerns that consumers may be rapidly exhausting their savings buffers
may therefore be overstated. Supporting this view, Bank of America Institute consumer
1 Bank of America Institute, “Consumer Checkpoint: Strength in numbers.” As of October 9, 2026.
2 Bank of America, “Massive Savings Markup = Another Reason to Be Bullish.” As of October 2, 2026.

Investment Implications
Consumer spending strength
remains a key pillar of economic
and earnings growth, which keeps
us constructive on our Equity
overweight. The growing connection
between household wealth and
spending reinforces the importance
of maintaining a diversified portfolio.

### Page 3

3 of 8 October 13, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
deposit account data show that middle- and lower-income households continue to maintain
deposit balances above prepandemic levels across age groups.
Third, elevated household wealth continues to provide a powerful tailwind for consumer
spending, particularly among households that account for an outsized share of overall
consumption. U.S. household net worth rose $12.8 trillion (T) to a record $195.9T in Q2,

driven largely by gains in Equities, which contributed $10.7T of the increase (Exhibit 1D).
As a result, household net worth relative to disposable income climbed to a record 8.3,
meaning households now hold more than eight dollars of wealth for every dollar of annual
disposable income. Households are also sitting on $35.8T in home equity, an all-time high
level that provides a significant balance sheet cushion. Rising asset values have helped to

support spending through what economists refer to as wealth effects. In other words, when
households feel wealthier, they tend to be more willing to spend.
For investors, the key takeaway is that consumer spending is being supported by multiple
engines despite the weaker sentiment data. Labor income trends are supporting wider
participation, healthy balance sheets are helping households absorb economic pressures,
and elevated household wealth continues to sustain higher-income spending. Together,

these forces are supportive of economic growth and corporate profits, which is particularly
important since the consumer accounts for roughly 70% of U.S. gross domestic product.
That said, it does not eliminate risk. A meaningful risk to the consumer would be a scenario
in which both the labor and wealth engines weaken simultaneously. For now, labor market
conditions remain stable, and wealth effects remain supportive. Equity market pullbacks in
recent years have done little to derail consumer spending, suggesting it would likely take a

deeper and more prolonged drawdown to alter consumer behavior.

MARKET VIEW
The Bull Market Turns Four: Older, Unloved—But Still Kicking
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
Ariana Chiu, Assistant Vice President and Investment Strategist
Four years ago this month, virtually no one was in the mood to buy U.S. Equities, and for
good reasons.
Rewind to October 2022: Inflation the month before clocked in at an annual rate of 8.2%.

The yield curve was inverted, a signal that has preceded most past recessions. Consumer
sentiment was abysmal, having dropped to a record low in June 2022. Recession
concerns were mounting, with an October Wall Street Journal poll survey putting the
odds of a recession within 12 months at about 63%. And realizing (belatedly) that
inflation was not “transitory,” the Fed was in the midst of administering one of the most
aggressive monetary tightening cycles in decades, raising the fed funds rate five times

between March and September 2022 by a stunning 300 basis points.
Reflecting the mood at the moment, the S&P 500 closed at 3,577 on October 12, 2022,
down 25% below its January peak, leaving investors battered and bruised but blind to the
fact that the market had bottomed. Four years on, the bull market that emerged from
the gloom of 2022 is still standing and kicking, with the S&P 500 up roughly 130% since
the October 2022 lows.
The more than doubling of the S&P over the past four years has hardly been linear—and

has been accompanied with plenty of pain and doubt. Exhibit 2 maps the rocky path
trodden by the bull over the past four years. Less than six months into the rally, the
markets were rocked by the failure of Silicon Valley Bank (SVB) on March 10, 2023. The
S&P Regional Banks Select Industry Index lost 18% that week and stoked concerns of
massive deposit runs and credit stress across the capital markets. However, owing to
emergency intervention by the government, lower yields and strength elsewhere in the

markets, the crisis passed. Some banks failed that month, but the bull market didn’t.
Exhibit 2: Revisiting the Rocky Path Trodden by the Bull.
Period "Reason to Sell" What happened instead
October 2022 Inflation >8%, aggressive Fed tightening, recession concerns Bull market begins
March 2023 SVB/regional banking crisis Stocks keep climbing
2023-24 Inverted yield curve, “recession inevitable” Economy keeps expanding
2024 “Magnificent 7* bubble,” narrow breadth, expensive valuations Earnings propel market higher

2025 Tariffs, trade war, policy uncertainty Market absorbs another shock
2026 Oil/diesel shock, 5%+ yields, Fed tightening, geopolitical risks Bull reaches fourth birthday
*Apple, Amazon, Alphabet, Nvidia, Meta, Microsoft and Tesla. Source: Chief Investment Office. As of October 9, 2026.
But recessionary concerns hung like threatening clouds over the markets for most of
2023. Aggressive Fed tightening, an inverted yield curve, and soft economic data were

all taken as harbingers of an economic slowdown and profits recession. Yet the S&P 500
continued to climb in 2023, up 26.3% for the year, driven in large part by the outsized
price performance of the Magnificent Seven.
Investors spent much of 2024 fretting about a market too dependent on technology,
and therefore a market too narrow and too expensive. The unwinding of the Japan carry
trade in August 2024 engendered more angst, as did uncertainties around the 2024

U.S. presidential election. But despite a host of misgivings, the S&P 500 delivered total
returns of 25% in 2024. The unloved bull marched on.
Total returns in 2025 were robust as well—roughly 18%—notwithstanding—yet
again—a number of headwinds. In late January, DeepSeek, a cheaper China AI model,
raised questions about America’s technology leadership and the need to spend enormous
sums on chips and data centers. The Nasdaq promptly dropped by 3.1% thereafter.

Investment Implications
The adoption phase of the AI buildout
should translate to broader earnings
growth and performance across
sectors, reinforcing our conviction
in U.S. Equities. Given a strong
fundamental backdrop, we would
continue to buy on weakness as the
bull enters its fifth year

### Page 5

5 of 8 October 13, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
January’s jolt was followed by “Liberation Day,” or the warning of punishing U.S. tariffs
on America’s major trading partners. The trade-policy shock of April 2025 caused the
S&P 500 to drop nearly 19% from its February high to its April 8 low on uncertainty
that higher import costs would decrease corporate margins and consumer spending and
upend global supply chains. Amid the trade tumult, Treasury prices fell and the U.S. dollar

weakened on concerns that foreigners were abandoning U.S. assets. Remember all the
angst over the dollar debasement trade? If that wasn’t enough, expensive valuations and
enormous capital commitments raised doubts about the AI trade and triggered talk of an
AI bubble. Yet despite this wall of worry, the S&P 500, for the third year running, posted
double-digit gains in 2025.
And double-digit gains could be in store this year, with the S&P 500 up 14% through

October 9. The bull has rumbled on, taking in stride the energy shock from the war in
the Middle East, the shift in interest rate expectations from the Fed (from rate cuts
to rate hikes), and the rising uncertainty associated with the midterm elections. These
headwinds have been countered by tailwinds such as better-than-expected corporate
earnings and better-than-anticipated real growth.
Finally, as Exhibit 3 makes clear, what has given this bull so much muscle has been

the U.S. Technology sector, specifically the Magnificent 7. Powering the bull has been
the Magnificent 7, with a staggering 344% return since the 2022 lows, while U.S.
technology has delivered a 277% return versus the S&P 500’s return of 130%. Note the
outperformance of the S&P 500 relative to the main international indices.
Looking forward: Is the bull’s greatest strength also its Achilles’ heel? Bull
markets don’t die of natural causes. And they typically don’t expire because of one

particular signal or indicator. Bull markets turn to bear markets when a cluster of signals/
metrics turn south. Think deteriorating and downward earnings revisions, higher interest
rates, widening credit spreads and tighter lending standards, growth disappointments,
stretched valuations and sentiment, deteriorating market breadth, and an external
shock. Any one of these signals could flash red and not trouble the bull.  But when they
converge or feed off each other, that’s trouble.

In addition, since a great deal of the earnings growth, market leadership and capital
expenditures (capex) spending of this bull market have come from the technology sector,
the bull’s greatest strength could become its greatest vulnerability. AI has become an
investment ecosystem unto itself, and hence the fifth year of the bull market will pivot
in part around AI revenue, frontier model adoption rates, infrastructure spending, free
cash flows, depreciation costs, debt issuance, data center utilization, cloud growth, and

competition via cheaper models and computing alternatives. To put it mildly, there is a
great deal at stake when it comes to the bull and AI.
However, this is where we stand today: We believe the bull will continue to rumble
ahead next year, with AI revenues and productivity gains justifying the costs of the AI
infrastructure. Critically, AI is moving from the buildout phase, dominated by chipmakers
and hyperscaler capex, into the adoption phase, where software firms, healthcare,

finance, energy and a myriad of other firms start to see productivity gains and margin
expansion from rising AI adoption rates. We believe this will give better breadth to the
S&P 500; so too will continued profit growth across various U.S. sectors, AI investment
that starts to pay off, and an economy capable of absorbing setbacks or periodic bouts
of uncertainty.
The path forward won’t be linear—and there will be fits and starts, and market
drawdowns. That’s normal for any market. But that aside, the bull will keep rumbling,

roaming and kicking in 2027.

### Page 6

6 of 8 October 13, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
THE TICKER TAPE
What is our view of the macro and market landscape heading
into 2027 and beyond?
Christopher Hyzy, Chief Investment Office
A supply side-led economic expansion continues, driven by capital investment in the AI
buildout, solid consumer spending and fiscal tailwinds still blowing, albeit with less force.
This creates a backdrop that keeps nominal growth well-above trend and, importantly,

above the interest cost on national debt.
The interest rate backdrop largely remains the same as of today, but, instead of the
sharp increases that we experienced in 2026, the curve should settle down, and yields
should remain relatively range bound. The Fed is likely to tap on the breaks a couple of
more times before reassessing new inflation, growth and employment trends later in
2027.
Concerns surrounding credit risk and "circular" financing are likely to rise the further we

move into the AI buildout, but we do not expect a default cycle to develop. Rather, we
expect the sharp backup in yields to place pressure namely on lower-quality credits in
areas having a difficult time building the cash flow necessary to pay the higher interest
costs.
We expect a broadening out within the market but technology to still maintain its
heavyweight positioning. As we move from training and evolving the large language
models to agentic, and physical AI new leaders are likely to develop. In addition, we

anticipate AI adopters such as biotechnology, transport, logistics, advance manufacturing
and financial companies to enter new growth and productivity phases.
Lastly, overall earnings growth in the U.S. should be low to mid double-digit percent
stages in 2027, which is about half of the growth from 2026 thus far but still around
double from normal levels.
Credit risks developing and higher energy prices for longer are the main risks to our bull
case for Equities extending further into 2027 and beyond.

Index Definitions
ecurities indexes assume reinvestment of all distributions and interest payments. Indexes are unmanaged and do not take into account fees or expenses. It is not possible to
invest directly in an index. Indexes are all based in U.S. dollars.
S&P 500 Index is a stock market index tracking the stock performance of 500 leading companies listed on stock exchanges in the United States.

University of Michigan’s Consumer Sentiment Index is a monthly survey of consumer confidence levels in the United States conducted by the University of Michigan.
Conference Board’s Index of Consumer Confidence measures what consumers are feeling about their expected financial situation, whether that's optimistic or pessimistic.
Consumer Price Index is a statistical measure that tracks the average change over time in the prices paid by urban consumers for a representative "market basket" of everyday goods and

services.
Nasdaq Composite Index is a stock market measure that tracks the performance of more than 3,000 stocks listed on the Nasdaq exchange.
MSCI Emerging Markets c is a stock market index that tracks the performance of large-cap and mid-cap companies across 24 developing and fast-growing nations.
MSCI All-Country World Index ex USA is a market-capitalization-weighted stock market index that tracks large- and mid-cap equity performance across developed and emerging markets

worldwide, excluding the United States.
MSCI Europe, Australasia, and the Far East (EAFE) Index is a stock market index that measures the performance of large- and mid-cap companies across 21 developed.
Dow Jones Industrial Average Index is a stock market index that tracks 30 major public companies listed on the New York Stock Exchange (NYSE) and Nasdaq.
S&P 500 Equal-Weighted Index is an alternative version of the standard S&P 500 that gives every constituent company an identical weight of 0.2% during each quarterly rebalance, rather

than weighting companies by their market capitalization.
Russell 2000 Index is a stock market index that tracks the performance of approximately 2,000 small-cap (small-capitalization) public companies in the United States.
S&P 500 sub-sectors and industry groups Global Industry Classification Standard (GICS®)/S&P 500 Total Return Index, including Information Technology Total Return (TR) USD;
Consumer Discretionary TR USD; Industrials TR USD; Real Estate TR USD; Communication Services TR USD; Materials  TR USD; Financials TR USD; Consumer Staples TR USD; Utilities TR USD;
